Asymptotic Properties of the Maximum Likelihood Estimator for Markov-switching Observation-driven Models
Frederik Bjerg Krabbe

Abstract
A Markov-switching observation-driven model is a stochastic process ( ( S t , Y t ) ) t ∈ ℤ ((S_{t},Y_{t}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}} where ( S t ) t ∈ ℤ (S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}} is an unobserved Markov chain on a finite set and ( Y t ) t ∈ ℤ (Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}} is an observed stochastic process such that the conditional distribution of Y t Y_{t} given ( Y τ ) τ ≤ t − 1 (Y_{\tau})_{\tau\leq t-1} and ( S τ ) τ ≤ t (S_{\tau})_{\tau\leq t} depends on ( Y τ ) τ ≤ t − 1 (Y_{\tau})_{\tau\leq t-1} and S t S_{t} . In this paper, we prove consistency and asymptotic normality of the maximum likelihood estimator for such model. As a special case, we also give conditions under which the maximum likelihood estimator for the widely applied Markov-switching generalised autoregressive conditional heteroscedasticity model introduced by Haas, Mittnik, and Paolella (2004b) is consistent and asymptotically normal. Keywords: State Space Models, Hidden Markov Models, Maximum Likelihood Estimation, Consistency, Asymptotic Normality, Markov-switching Generalised Autoregressive Conditional Heteroscedasticity Models. JEL Classifications: C12, C13, C22, C32, C58.



I am grateful to Leopoldo Catania, Morten Ørregaard Nielsen, Christian Francq, Jean-Michel Zakoïan, Christian Gourieroux, and Anders Rahbek, seminar participants at CREST, and conference participants at the 2024 Virtual Workshop for Junior Researchers in Time Series, the 2024 Quantitative Finance and Financial Econometrics International Conference, the 2025 Italian Congress of Econometrics and Empirical Economics, and the 2025 Annual Society for Financial Econometrics Conference for comments and discussions. This research is supported by the Danish National Research Foundation (DNRF Chair grant number DNRF154).

1
Introduction

State space models and their extensions are ubiquitous in economics and finance. A state space model is a stochastic process
((X_{t},Y_{t}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
where
(X_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an unobserved Markov process taking values in
X
and
(Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an observed stochastic process taking values in
Y
such that the conditional distribution of
Y_{t}
given
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
where
\mathbf{Y}_{i}^{j}:=(Y_{i},...,Y_{j})
and
\mathbf{X}_{-\infty}^{t}
where
\mathbf{X}_{i}^{j}:=(X_{i},...,X_{j})
depends only on
X_{t}
. It is called a hidden Markov model when
X_{t}=S_{t}
and
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a Markov chain on a finite set.

The arguably most well-known extension of the state space model is the autoregressive state space model of order
p\in\mathbb{N}
in which the conditional distribution of
Y_{t}
given
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
and
\mathbf{X}_{-\infty}^{t}
depends on both
\mathbf{Y}_{t-p}^{t-1}
and
X_{t}
. An example of an autoregressive state space model is the seminal Markov-switching autoregressive model introduced by
Hamilton (1989)
to model economic growth where
X
is finite. Another example is the Markov-switching autoregressive conditional heteroscedasticity (ARCH) model introduced independently by
Cai (1994)
and
Hamilton and Susmel (1994)
to model financial returns where
X
is also finite. See, for instance,
Hamilton (2010)
and
Ang and Timmermann (2012)
for more examples of autoregressive state space models in economics and finance, respectively.

Another extension of the state space model that has gained popularity recently is the observation-driven state space model in which the conditional distribution of
Y_{t}
given
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
and
\mathbf{X}_{-\infty}^{t}
now depends on both
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
and
X_{t}
. An example of an observation-driven state space model is the Markov-switching generalised ARCH (GARCH) model introduced by
Haas et al. (2004b)
where
X
is finite.
1
1

1

Note that there exist two types of Markov-switching GARCH models namely the one considered by
Francq et al. (2001)
in which the conditional distribution of
Y_{t}
given
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
and
\mathbf{X}_{-\infty}^{t}
depends on both
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
and
\mathbf{X}_{-\infty}^{t}
and the one by
Haas et al. (2004b)
just discussed. See Section
2.2
for details.
Yet another example from finance is the score-driven state space model introduced by
Delle Monache et al. (2021)
where
X
is not finite. For examples of observation-driven state space models in economics, see, for instance,
Delle Monache et al. (2016)
and
Angelini and Gorgi (2018)
where
X
is not finite. For more examples of observation-driven state space models in finance, see, for instance,
Haas et al. (2004a)
,
Ardia (2009)
,
Broda et al. (2013)
,
Ardia et al. (2018)
,
Haas and Liu (2018)
,
Bernardi and Catania (2019)
, and
Walden (2019)
where
X
is finite or
Buccheri et al. (2021)
and
Buccheri and Corsi (2021)
where
X
is not finite.

Statistical inference for state space models and their extensions – including estimation, which is typically done by maximum likelihood estimation – is therefore of significant practical importance. The asymptotic properties of the maximum likelihood estimator (MLE) for observation-driven state space models have, however, attracted almost no attention in the literature despite the increasing popularity of such models. In this paper, we prove both consistency (Theorem
2
) and asymptotic normality (Theorem
3
) of the MLE for an observation-driven state space model where
X
is finite, called a Markov-switching observation-driven model. To the best of our knowledge, these results are the first of their kind in the literature. As a special case, we also give conditions under which the MLE for the widely applied Markov-switching GARCH model by
Haas et al. (2004b)
is both consistent (Theorem
5
) and asymptotically normal (Theorem
7
). Again, this is new to the literature and extends
Kandji and Misko (2024)
, who gave conditions under which it is only consistent.

In contrast, the asymptotic properties of the MLE for autoregressive state space models, that is, the models in which the conditional distribution of
Y_{t}
given
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
and
\mathbf{X}_{-\infty}^{t}
depends
only
on
\mathbf{Y}_{t-p}^{t-1}
and
X_{t}
, have attracted much attention in the literature. For autoregressive state space models where
X
is finite, consistency of the MLE was proved by
Francq and Roussignol (1998)
,
Krishnamurthy and Rydén (1998)
, and
Francq et al. (2001)
. This was later generalised by
Douc et al. (2004)
, who proved consistency and asymptotic normality of the MLE for autoregressive state space models where
X
is compact and not necessarily finite, a seminal result which can be used to give conditions under which both the MLE for the Markov-switching autoregressive model and the Markov-switching ARCH model is consistent and asymptotically normal. More recently,
Kasahara and Shimotsu (2019)
relax some of the assumptions in
Douc et al. (2004)
.
2
2

2

Consistency and asymptotic normality of the MLE for hidden Markov models was proved by
Leroux (1992)
and
Bickel et al. (1998)
, respectively. Local consistency and asymptotic normality of the MLE for state space models where
X
is compact was proved by
Jensen and Petersen (1999)
, and global consistency of the MLE for general state space models was proved by
Douc et al. (2011)
. See
Douc et al. (2011)
for more references on the asymptotic properties of the MLE for state space models.
In comparison to
Douc et al. (2004)
and
Kasahara and Shimotsu (2019)
, we prove consistency and asymptotic normality of the MLE for models in which the conditional distribution of
Y_{t}
given
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
and
\mathbf{X}_{-\infty}^{t}
depends on
both

\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
and
X_{t}
, but with
X
finite. This result can, in contrast to the ones in
Douc et al. (2004)
and
Kasahara and Shimotsu (2019)
, be used to give conditions under which also the MLE for the widely applied Markov-switching GARCH model by
Haas et al. (2004b)
is consistent and asymptotically normal.

In the proofs of consistency and asymptotic normality of the MLE for the model, the fact that the time-varying parameters and the filter forget their initialisations asymptotically (Lemmas
1
and
2
, respectively) is crucial, similarly to
Douc et al. (2004)
(Corollary 1) and
Kasahara and Shimotsu (2019)
(Lemma 1). However, differently from
Douc et al. (2004)
and
Kasahara and Shimotsu (2019)
, who use theory for Markov chains, we prove these results using theory for stochastic difference equations since the latter gives lower-level conditions under which the MLE for the model is consistent and asymptotically normal, which are easier to verify. Theory for stochastic difference equations is also usually used for standard observation-driven models, see, for instance,
Berkes et al. (2003)
,
Francq and Zakoïan (2004)
,
Straumann and Mikosch (2006)
,
Blasques et al. (2018)
, and
Blasques et al. (2022)
.

The rest of the paper is organised as follows. Section
2
introduces the Markov-switching observation-driven model, and Section
3
gives some examples of Markov-switching observation-driven models. In Section
4
, the probabilistic properties of the model is studied. The asymptotic properties of the MLE for the model is then studied in Section
5
. Section
6
studies both the asymptotic and finite-sample properties of the MLE for the Markov-switching GARCH model by
Haas et al. (2004b)
, the latter in a Monte Carlo simulation study. Section
7
concludes. All proofs except the ones in the main text are collected in the appendix.

2
The Markov-switching Observation-driven Model

2.1
Model

A Markov-switching observation-driven model is a stochastic process
((S_{t},Y_{t}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
where
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an unobserved Markov chain taking values in
\{1,...,J\}
with transition probabilities

p_{ij}:=\mathbb{P}(S_{t+1}=j\mid S_{t}=i),\quad i,j\in\{1,...,J\},

and
(Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an observed stochastic process taking values in
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
such that the conditional distribution of
Y_{t}
given
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
and
\mathbf{S}_{-\infty}^{t}
depends only on
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
and
S_{t}
as follows

Y_{t}\mid(\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1},S_{t})\sim\mathcal{D}_{S_{t}}(X_{S_{t},t},\bm{\upsilon}_{S_{t}}),

where
X_{j,t},j\in\{1,...,J\}
is a time-varying parameter taking values in a complete set
\mathcal{X}_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
given by

X_{j,t+1}=\phi_{j}(Y_{t},X_{j,t};\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),

and
\bm{\upsilon}_{j},j\in\{1,...,J\}
is a vector of constant parameters taking values in a set
\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{j}}
. If
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) chain, that is, if

p_{1j}=\cdots=p_{Jj}

for all
j\in\{1,...,J\}
, then the Markov-switching observation-driven model is called a mixture observation-driven model.

In the Markov-switching observation-driven model, filtering, prediction, and smoothing of the unobserved Markov chain
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
, that is, computation of the conditional distribution

\pi_{j,t\mid s}:=\mathbb{P}(S_{t}=j\mid\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{s}),\quad j\in\{1,...,J\},

which is called the filtering distribution when
t=s
, the predictive distribution when
t>s
, and the smoothing distribution when
t<s
, is done as in the Markov-switching autoregressive model and the hidden Markov model. The one-step-ahead prediction is given by

\pi_{j,t+1\mid t}=\sum_{i=1}^{J}p_{ij}\pi_{i,t\mid t},

and the filter is given by

\pi_{j,t\mid t}=\frac{\pi_{j,t\mid t-1}f_{j}(Y_{t};X_{j,t},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}{f(Y_{t})},

where
f(y),y\in\mathcal{Y}
is the conditional probability density function (pdf) of
Y_{t}
given
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
given by

f(y)=\sum_{k=1}^{J}\pi_{k,t\mid t-1}f_{k}(y;X_{k,t},\bm{\upsilon}_{k}),

and
f_{j}(y;X_{j,t},\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),y\in\mathcal{Y}
is the conditional pdf of
Y_{t}
given
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
and
S_{t}=j
, see
Hamilton (1994)
for details.
3
3

3

More generally, the
h
-step-ahead prediction is given by
\pi_{j,t+h\mid t}=\sum_{i=1}^{J}p_{ij}^{(h)}\pi_{i,t\mid t}
where
p_{ij}^{(h)}:=\mathbb{P}(S_{t+h}=j\mid S_{t}=i)
.
Let
\bm{\pi}_{t\mid s}:=(\pi_{1,t\mid s},...,\pi_{J,t\mid s})^{\prime}
. Then,

\bm{\pi}_{t+1\mid t}=\mathbf{P}^{\prime}\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t},

where
\mathbf{P}
is the transition probability matrix given by

\mathbf{P}:=\begin{bmatrix}p_{11}&\cdots&p_{1J}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
p_{J1}&\cdots&p_{JJ}\\
\end{bmatrix},

and

\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t}=\mathbf{F}_{t}(\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1})\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1},

where
\mathbf{F}_{t}(\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1})
is a diagonal matrix with generic element

[\mathbf{F}_{t}(\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1})]_{ii}:=\frac{f_{i}(Y_{t};X_{i,t},\bm{\upsilon}_{i})}{\sum_{k=1}^{J}\pi_{k,t\mid t-1}f_{k}(Y_{t};X_{k,t},\bm{\upsilon}_{k})},\quad i\in\{1,...,J\}.

Moreover, the smoother is given by

\pi_{j,t\mid T}=\pi_{j,t\mid t}\sum_{i=1}^{J}p_{ji}\frac{\pi_{i,t+1\mid T}}{\pi_{i,t+1\mid t}},\quad t<T,

see
Hamilton (1994)
for details once again. Prediction of the observed stochastic process
(Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is also done as in the Markov-switching autoregressive model and the hidden Markov model.

Finally, note that the Markov-switching observation-driven model reduces to the Markov-switching autoregressive model of order
1
if
\phi_{j}(Y_{t},X_{j,t};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})=\phi_{j}(Y_{t};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})
for all
j\in\{1,...,J\}
and to the hidden Markov model if
X_{j,t}\equiv X_{j}
for all
j\in\{1,...,J\}
.
4
4

4

All results continue to hold in the case where
X_{j,t+1}=\phi_{j}(Y_{t},...,Y_{t-p+1},X_{j,t};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})
– in which case the Markov-switching observation-driven model reduces to the Markov-switching autoregressive model of order
p\in\mathbb{N}
if
\phi_{j}(Y_{t},...,Y_{t-p+1},X_{j,t};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})=\phi_{j}(Y_{t},...,Y_{t-p+1};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})
for all
j\in\{1,...,J\}
– but, to ease the exposition, we consider the case where
X_{j,t+1}=\phi_{j}(Y_{t},X_{j,t};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})
.

2.2
Terminology

A word on terminology. Let
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
be an unobserved Markov chain. In the previous section, we defined a Markov-switching observation-driven model as a stochastic process
((S_{t},Y_{t}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
where
(Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an observed stochastic process such that the conditional distribution of
Y_{t}
given
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
and
\mathbf{S}_{-\infty}^{t}
depends only on
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
and
S_{t}
as follows

Y_{t}\mid(\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1},S_{t})\sim\mathcal{D}_{S_{t}}(X_{S_{t},t},\bm{\upsilon}_{S_{t}}),

(1)

with

X_{S_{t},t}=\phi_{S_{t}}(Y_{t-1},X_{S_{t},t-1};\bm{\upsilon}_{S_{t}});

(2)

its dependence structure is illustrated in Figure
1
. As mentioned in the introduction, the Markov-switching GARCH model by
Haas et al. (2004b)
is an example of such a model, see the next section for details. In such a Markov-switching observation-driven model, filtering, prediction, and smoothing of the unobserved Markov chain
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
and thus prediction of the observed stochastic process
(Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
can be done via the formulae in the previous section.

\dots
S_{t-1}
S_{t}
\dots
\dots
Y_{t-1}
Y_{t}
\dots

Figure 1
:
The dependence structure of the Markov-switching observation-driven model in Equations (
1
) and (
2
).

One could, however, also have defined a Markov-switching observation-driven model as a stochastic process
((S_{t},Y_{t}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
where
(Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an observed stochastic process such that the conditional distribution of
Y_{t}
given
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
and
\mathbf{S}_{-\infty}^{t}
depends on both
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1}
and
\mathbf{S}_{-\infty}^{t}
as follows

Y_{t}\mid(\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-1},\mathbf{S}_{-\infty}^{t})\sim\mathcal{D}_{S_{t}}(X_{S_{t},t},\bm{\upsilon}_{S_{t}}),

(3)

with

X_{S_{t},t}=\phi_{S_{t}}(Y_{t-1},X_{S_{t-1},t-1};\bm{\upsilon}_{S_{t}}).

(4)

The Markov-switching GARCH model considered by
Francq et al. (2001)
is an example of such a model. The dependence structure of such a Markov-switching observation-driven model is illustrated in Figure
2
. In such a model, filtering, prediction, and smoothing of the unobserved Markov chain
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
can, however, not be done due to the so-called path-dependence problem, a problem which makes such a model complicated, if not close to impossible, to handle. We stress that we consider the former, and
not
the latter, Markov-switching observation-driven model.

\dots
S_{t-1}
S_{t}
\dots
\dots
Y_{t-1}
Y_{t}
\dots

Figure 2
:
The dependence structure of the Markov-switching observation-driven model in Equations (
3
) and (
4
).

3
Examples of Markov-switching Observation-driven Models

In this section, we give some examples of Markov-switching observation-driven models.

Example 1
.

An example of a Markov-switching observation-driven model is

Y_{t}=X_{S_{t},t}+\sigma_{S_{t}}\varepsilon_{t},

(5)

where
(\varepsilon_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence of independent standard normal distributed random variables independent of
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
and, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

X_{j,t+1}=\omega_{j}+\alpha_{j}Y_{t}+\beta_{j}X_{j,t},

where
\omega_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\beta_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\sigma_{j}^{2}>0
. Here,
\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{D}_{S_{t}}
is the normal distribution with mean
X_{S_{t},t}
and variance
\sigma_{S_{t}}^{2}
,
\mathcal{X}_{j}=\mathbb{R}
,
\phi_{j}(y,x_{j};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})=[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{1}+[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{2}y+[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{3}x_{j}
,
\bm{\upsilon}_{j}=(\omega_{j},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},\sigma_{j}^{2})^{\prime}
, and
\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)
.

A related model is the Markov-switching autoregressive model of order
p\in\mathbb{N}
by
Hamilton (1989)
. This model is given by

Y_{t}=a_{S_{t}}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}b_{S_{t}}^{(i)}Y_{t-i}+\sigma_{S_{t}}\varepsilon_{t},

where
(\varepsilon_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence of independent standard normal distributed random variables independent of
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
as above.

It can be shown that if
(Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is stationary and ergodic with
\mathbb{E}[\log^{+}|Y_{t}|]<\infty
where
\log^{+}x:=\max(\log x,0)
for all
x>0
and
|\beta_{j}|<1
for all
j\in\{1,...,J\}
, then

X_{j,t}=\frac{\omega_{j}}{1-\beta_{j}}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{j}\beta_{j}^{i-1}Y_{t-i}

for all
j\in\{1,...,J\}
. The Markov-switching observation-driven model in Equation (
5
) can thus be thought of as a Markov-switching autoregressive model of order infinity given by

Y_{t}=a_{S_{t}}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}b_{S_{t}}^{(i)}Y_{t-i}+\sigma_{S_{t}}\varepsilon_{t},

where

a_{S_{t}}:=\frac{\omega_{S_{t}}}{1-\beta_{S_{t}}}\quad\text{and}\quad b_{S_{t}}^{(i)}:=\alpha_{S_{t}}\beta_{S_{t}}^{i-1}.

Example 2
.

The Markov-switching GARCH model by
Haas et al. (2004b)
is given by

Y_{t}=\sqrt{X_{S_{t},t}}\varepsilon_{t},

where
(\varepsilon_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence of independent standard normal distributed random variables independent of
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
and, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

X_{j,t+1}=\omega_{j}+\alpha_{j}Y_{t}^{2}+\beta_{j}X_{j,t},

where
\omega_{j}>0
,
\alpha_{j}\geq 0
, and
\beta_{j}\geq 0
. This is also an example of a Markov-switching observation-driven model where
\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{D}_{S_{t}}
is the normal distribution with mean zero and variance
X_{S_{t},t}
,
\mathcal{X}_{j}=\left[0,\infty\right)
,
\phi_{j}(y,x_{j};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})=[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{1}+[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{2}y^{2}+[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{3}x_{j}
,
\bm{\upsilon}_{j}=(\omega_{j},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j})^{\prime}
, and
\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}=[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)
. Note that it reduces to the mixture GARCH model by
Haas et al. (2004a)
if
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an i.i.d. chain.

The Markov-switching GARCH model is not the only Markov-switching conditional heteroscedasticity model that the Markov-switching observation-driven model nests. Consider, for instance, the Markov-switching asymmetric power GARCH model given by

Y_{t}=X_{S_{t},t}^{\frac{1}{\delta}}\varepsilon_{t},\quad\delta>0,

where
(\varepsilon_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is as above and, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

X_{j,t+1}=\omega_{j}+\alpha_{j}\left(\left|Y_{t}\right|-\xi_{j}Y_{t}\right)^{\delta}+\beta_{j}X_{j,t},

where
\omega_{j}>0
,
\alpha_{j}\geq 0
,
|\xi_{j}|\leq 1
, and
\beta_{j}\geq 0
, which reduces to the Markov-switching GJR-GARCH model if
\delta=2
, the Markov-switching threshold GARCH model if
\delta=1
, and the standard Markov-switching GARCH model above if
\delta=2
and
\xi_{j}=0
for all
j\in\{1,...,J\}
. This is also an example of a Markov-switching observation-driven model where
\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{D}_{S_{t}}
is the normal distribution with mean zero and variance
X_{S_{t},t}^{\frac{2}{\delta}}
,
\mathcal{X}_{j}=\left[0,\infty\right)
,
\phi_{j}(y,x_{j};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})=[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{1}+[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{2}(|y|-[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{3}y)^{\delta}+[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{4}x_{j}
,
\bm{\upsilon}_{j}=(\omega_{j},\alpha_{j},\xi_{j},\beta_{j})^{\prime}
, and
\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}=[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times[-1,1]\times[0,\infty)
.

Example 3
.

Let
y\mapsto F(y;x,\tilde{\bm{\upsilon}})
be a cumulative distribution function (cdf) with support
\mathcal{N}\subseteq[0,\infty)
, for instance,
\mathcal{N}=\{0,1\}
,
\mathcal{N}=\mathbb{N}
, or
\mathcal{N}=[0,1]
, indexed by the mean
x
and a vector of parameters
\tilde{\bm{\upsilon}}
, and assume that

x\leq x^{*}\quad\Rightarrow\quad F^{-}(u;x,\tilde{\bm{\upsilon}})\leq F^{-}(u;x^{*},\tilde{\bm{\upsilon}})

for all
u\in(0,1)
where
F^{-}(u;x,\tilde{\bm{\upsilon}}):=\inf\{y\in\mathcal{N}:F(y;x,\tilde{\bm{\upsilon}})\geq u\}
.

The (present-regime dependent) Markov-switching positive linear conditional mean model by
Aknouche and Francq (2022)
is given by

Y_{t}=F^{-}_{S_{t}}(U_{t};X_{S_{t},t},\tilde{\bm{\upsilon}}_{S_{t}}),

where
(U_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence of independent uniform distributed random variables on
[0,1]
independent of
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
and, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

X_{j,t+1}=\omega_{j}+\alpha_{j}Y_{t}+\beta_{j}X_{j,t},

where
\omega_{j}>0
,
\alpha_{j}\geq 0
,
\beta_{j}\geq 0
, and
\tilde{\bm{\upsilon}}_{j}\in\tilde{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}
. This is another example of a Markov-switching observation-driven model where
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{N}
,
F_{S_{t}}
is the cdf of
\mathcal{D}_{S_{t}}
,
\mathcal{X}_{j}=\left[0,\infty\right)
,
\phi_{j}(y,x_{j};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})=[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{1}+[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{2}y+[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{3}x_{j}
,
\bm{\upsilon}_{j}=(\omega_{j},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},\tilde{\bm{\upsilon}}_{j})^{\prime}
, and
\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}=[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)\times\tilde{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}
.

Example 4
.

A random variable
Y
with support
[-\pi,\pi]
is said to be von Mises distributed with location
x\in[-\pi,\pi]
and concentration
\tilde{\upsilon}>0
if the pdf of
Y
is given by

f(y;x,\tilde{\upsilon})=\frac{1}{2\pi I_{0}(\tilde{\upsilon})}\exp(\tilde{\upsilon}\cos(y-x)),\quad y\in[-\pi,\pi],

where
I_{0}(\cdot)
is the modified Bessel function of the first kind of order 0.

Yet another example of a Markov-switching observation-driven model is the Markov-switching score-driven model

Y_{t}=\left(X_{S_{t},t}+\varepsilon_{S_{t},t}\right)\textup{mod}\,(2\pi)-\pi,

where, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,
(\varepsilon_{j,t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence of independent von Mises distributed random variables with location
0
and concentration
\tilde{\upsilon}_{j}>0
, which is independent of
(\varepsilon_{i,t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
for all
i\in\{1,...,J\}
such that
i\neq j
and
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
, and

X_{j,t+1}=\omega_{j}+\alpha_{j}\tilde{\upsilon}_{j}\sin\left(Y_{t}-X_{j,t}\right)+\beta_{j}X_{j,t},

where
\omega_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\beta_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
. The model is similar to the regime-switching score-driven model by
Harvey and Palumbo (2023)
, which is a generalisation of the score-driven model by
Harvey et al. (2024)
. Indeed, the Markov-switching score-driven model is a Markov-switching observation-driven model where
\mathcal{Y}=[-\pi,\pi]
,
\mathcal{D}_{S_{t}}
is the von Mises distribution with location
X_{S_{t},t}
and concentration
\tilde{\upsilon}_{S_{t}}
,
\mathcal{X}_{j}=\mathbb{R}
,
\phi_{j}(y,x_{j};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})=[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{1}+[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{2}[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{4}\sin(y-x_{j})+[\bm{\upsilon}_{j}]_{3}x_{j}
,
\bm{\upsilon}_{j}=(\omega_{j},\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},\tilde{\upsilon}_{j})^{\prime}
, and
\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)
.

4
Probabilistic Properties of the Model

First, we study the probabilistic properties of the Markov-switching observation-driven model. We restrict our attention to the Markov-switching observation-driven models that can be written as

Y_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{S_{t}}\mathbf{g}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{t};\mathbf{X}_{t},\bm{\upsilon}).

(6)

Here,
\mathbf{1}_{S_{t}}:=(1_{\{S_{t}=1\}},...,1_{\{S_{t}=J\}})
where
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a stationary, irreducible, and aperiodic (thus ergodic) Markov chain taking values in
\{1,...,J\}
with transition probabilities
p_{ij}
. Moreover,
\mathbf{g}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{t};\mathbf{X}_{t},\bm{\upsilon}):=(g_{1}(\varepsilon_{1,t};X_{1,t},\bm{\upsilon}_{1}),...,g_{J}(\varepsilon_{J,t};X_{J,t},\bm{\upsilon}_{J}))^{\prime}
where
\bm{\varepsilon}_{t}:=(\varepsilon_{1,t},...,\varepsilon_{J,t})^{\prime}
, for all
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,
(\varepsilon_{j,t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence of i.i.d. random variables taking values in
\mathcal{E}_{j}
with distribution
\mathcal{D}_{j}^{\varepsilon}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})
, which is independent of
(\varepsilon_{i,t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
for all
i\in\{1,...,J\}
such that
i\neq j
,
\mathbf{X}_{t}:=(X_{1,t},...,X_{J,t})^{\prime}
is given by

\mathbf{X}_{t+1}=\bm{\phi}(S_{t},\bm{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{X}_{t};\bm{\upsilon})

with

[\bm{\phi}(S_{t},\bm{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{X}_{t};\bm{\upsilon})]_{j}:=\phi_{j}(\mathbf{1}_{S_{t}}\mathbf{g}(\bm{\varepsilon}_{t};\mathbf{X}_{t},\bm{\upsilon}),X_{j,t};\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),\quad j\in\{1,...,J\},

and
\bm{\upsilon}:=(\bm{\upsilon}_{1},...,\bm{\upsilon}_{J})^{\prime}
. Finally,
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
(\varepsilon_{j,t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
are independent for all
j\in\{1,...,J\}
.
5
5

5

All examples in Section
3
can be written like this because if
\varepsilon_{i,t}\overset{d}{=}\varepsilon_{j,t}
for all
i,j\in\{1,...,J\}
, then let
\varepsilon_{j,t}=\varepsilon_{t}
for all
j\in\{1,...,J\}
where
(\varepsilon_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence of i.i.d. random variables taking values in
\mathcal{E}
with distribution
\mathcal{D}^{\varepsilon}(\bm{\upsilon})
.

4.1
Stationarity and Ergodicity

Theorem
1
, which follows from an application of Theorem 3.1 in
Bougerol (1993)
, gives conditions under which the model is stationary and ergodic. Let

\Lambda(\bm{\phi}_{t}):=\sup_{\underset{\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{X}_{J}}}\frac{||\bm{\phi}_{t}(\mathbf{x})-\bm{\phi}_{t}(\mathbf{y})||_{2}}{||\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}||_{2}},

where
\bm{\phi}_{t}(\mathbf{x}):=\bm{\phi}(S_{t},\bm{\varepsilon}_{t},\mathbf{x};\bm{\upsilon})
; here and in the following,
||\mathbf{x}||_{p}:=(\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x_{i}|^{p})^{1/p},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Theorem 1
.

Assume that

(i)

there exists an
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{X}_{J}
such that
\mathbb{E}[\log^{+}||\bm{\phi}_{t}(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{x}||_{2}]<\infty
,

(ii)

\mathbb{E}[\log^{+}\Lambda(\bm{\phi}_{t})]<\infty
, and

(iii)

there exists an
r\in\mathbb{N}
such that

-\infty\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\log\Lambda\left(\bm{\phi}_{t}^{(r)}\right)\right]<0,

where
\bm{\phi}_{t}^{(r)}(\mathbf{x}):=\bm{\phi}_{t}\circ\cdots\circ\bm{\phi}_{t-r+1}(\mathbf{x})
.

Then,
(Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is stationary and ergodic.

Conditions (i) and (ii) are standard regularity conditions, and Condition (iii) is a standard contraction condition – not in the strict sense, but in expectation.

5
Asymptotic Properties of the Maximum Likelihood Estimator

We now study the asymptotic properties of the MLE for the Markov-switching observation-driven model discussed in the previous section.

Assume that a sample
(Y_{t})_{t=1}^{T}
from the Markov-switching observation-driven model
(Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
given by Equation (
6
) with
\bm{\theta}=\bm{\theta}_{0}
is observed. Here,

\bm{\theta}:=(p_{ij},i=1,...,J,j=1,...,J-1,\bm{\upsilon}_{j},j=1,...,J)^{\prime}

is the parameter vector since
\sum_{j=1}^{J}p_{ij}=1
for all
i\in\{1,...,J\}
and

\bm{\Theta}\subset\left\{\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:p_{ij}>0,i=1,...,J,j=1,...,J-1,\sum_{j=1}^{J-1}p_{ij}<1,i=1,...,J,\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j},j=1,...,J\right\}

with
d:=J(J-1)+\sum_{j=1}^{J}d_{j}
is the parameter space. The MLE
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{T}
of
\bm{\theta}_{0}
is given by

\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{T}=\underset{\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}}{\arg\max}\,\hat{L}_{T}(\bm{\theta}).

Here,
\hat{L}_{T}(\bm{\theta})
is the
initialised
and thus non-stationary log-likelihood function given by

\hat{L}_{T}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\log\hat{f}(Y_{t};\bm{\theta})

with

\hat{f}(Y_{t};\bm{\theta})=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\hat{\pi}_{j,t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta})f_{j}(Y_{t};\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),

where, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,
(\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}))_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
is given by

\hat{X}_{j,t+1}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})=\phi_{j}(Y_{t},\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})

for some initialisation
\hat{X}_{j,1}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\in\mathcal{X}_{j}
and
(\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta}))_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
is given by

\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{t+1\mid t}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbf{P}^{\prime}\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{t\mid t}(\bm{\theta})

with

\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{t\mid t}(\bm{\theta})=\hat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}(\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta});\bm{\theta})\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta})

for some initialisation
\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{0\mid 0}(\bm{\theta})\in\mathcal{S}
with
\mathcal{S}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{J}:x_{j}\geq 0,j=1,...,J,\sum_{j=1}^{J}x_{j}=1\}
where

[\hat{\mathbf{F}}_{t}(\mathbf{s})]_{ii}:=\frac{f_{i}(Y_{t};\hat{X}_{i,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{i}),\bm{\upsilon}_{i})}{\sum_{k=1}^{J}s_{k}f_{k}(Y_{t};\hat{X}_{k,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k}),\bm{\upsilon}_{k})},\quad i\in\{1,...,J\}.

5.1
Consistency

Consistency follows from classical arguments if the
initialised
and thus non-stationary log-likelihood function
\hat{L}_{T}(\bm{\theta})
converges uniformly almost surely (a.s.) to a function, say,
L(\bm{\theta})
, which is uniquely maximised at
\bm{\theta}_{0}
. We show that
\hat{L}_{T}(\bm{\theta})
converges uniformly a.s. to
L(\bm{\theta})
in two steps, the first step being the main difficulty and hence focus. First, we show that the difference between
\hat{L}_{T}(\bm{\theta})
and the
non-initialised
, stationary, and ergodic log-likelihood function

L_{T}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\log f(Y_{t};\bm{\theta})

with

f(Y_{t};\bm{\theta})=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\pi_{j,t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta})f_{j}(Y_{t};X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),

where, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,
(X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is given in Lemma
1
and
(\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is given in Corollary
1
converges uniformly a.s. to zero by showing that the time-varying parameters and the predictor forget their initialisations asymptotically, that is, that, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
, the difference between
(\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}))_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
and the one between
(\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta}))_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
converge uniformly exponentially fast a.s. (e.a.s.) to zero.
6
6

6

A sequence of random matrices
(\mathbf{Z}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is said to converge to zero e.a.s. if there exists a
\gamma>1
such that
\gamma^{t}||\mathbf{Z}_{t}||_{p,p}\overset{a.s.}{\rightarrow}0\quad\text{as}\quad t\rightarrow\infty
where
||\mathbf{X}||_{p,p}:=(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{m}|x_{ij}|^{p})^{1/p},\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
.
Then, we show that
L_{T}(\bm{\theta})
converges uniformly a.s. to

L(\bm{\theta})=\mathbb{E}[\log f(Y_{t};\bm{\theta})]

by using standard arguments. We also show that
L(\bm{\theta})
is uniquely maximised at
\bm{\theta}_{0}
by using standard arguments.

We assume the following.

Assumption 1
.

The conditions in Theorem
1
hold for
\bm{\theta}=\bm{\theta}_{0}
.

Assumption 2
.

\bm{\Theta}
is compact.

Assumption 3
.

For each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

(i)

(x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\mapsto f_{j}(y;x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})
is continuous for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
and

(ii)

x_{j}\mapsto f_{j}(y;x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})
is differentiable for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
and
\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}
.

Assumption 4
.

For each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

(i)

(x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\mapsto\phi_{j}(y,x_{j};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})
is continuous for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
and

(ii)

x_{j}\mapsto\phi_{j}(y,x_{j};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})
is differentiable for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
and
\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}
.

Assumption
1
implies that
(Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is stationary and ergodic. Assumption
2
is a standard regularity condition. So are Assumptions
3
and
4
; Condition (i) in Assumption
3
is similar to Assumption 4 in
Douc et al. (2004)
and Assumption 6(a) in
Kasahara and Shimotsu (2019)
, Condition (ii) in Assumption
3
is an extra regularity condition, which is used to prove that the filter/predictor forgets its initialisation asymptotically, and Assumption
4
is similar to the assumptions in
Blasques et al. (2018)
.

The following lemma, which follows from an application of Theorem 3.1 in
Bougerol (1993)
, gives conditions under which, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
, the difference between
(\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}))_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
converges uniformly e.a.s. to zero. For each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
, let

\Lambda_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}):=\sup_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}}\left|\nabla_{x_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},x_{j};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|.

Lemma 1
.

Assume that Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
4
hold. Moreover, assume that, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

(i)

there exists an
x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}
such that
\mathbb{E}[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}|\phi_{j}(Y_{t},x_{j};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})-x_{j}|]<\infty
,

(ii)

\mathbb{E}[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}\Lambda_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})]<\infty
, and

(iii)

-\infty\leq\mathbb{E}[\log\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}\Lambda_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})]<0
.

Then, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,
(X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
given by

X_{j,t+1}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})=\phi_{j}(Y_{t},X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})

is stationary and ergodic for all
\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}
and

\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}\left|\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})-X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\overset{e.a.s.}{\rightarrow}0\quad\text{as}\quad t\rightarrow\infty

for any initialisation
\hat{X}_{j,1}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\in\mathcal{X}_{j}
.

As above, Conditions (i) and (ii) are standard regularity conditions, and Condition (iii) is a standard contraction condition. They are similar to the conditions in
Blasques et al. (2018)
.

Theorem 3.1 in
Bougerol (1993)
can, however, not be used for
(\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta}))_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
because
(\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta}))_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
depends on
(\hat{X}_{1,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{1}))_{t\in\mathbb{N}},...,(\hat{X}_{J,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{J}))_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
which are non-stationary. The following lemma, which gives conditions under which the difference between
(\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{t\mid t}(\bm{\theta}))_{t\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
and
(\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t}(\bm{\theta}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
converges uniformly e.a.s. to zero, follows instead from an application of Propositions 1 and 3 in
Krabbe (2025)
, which, when combined, is a generalisation of Theorem 2.10 in
Straumann and Mikosch (2006)
.
7
7

7

Propositions 1 and 3 in
Krabbe (2025)
generalise the result in Theorem 2.10 in
Straumann and Mikosch (2006)
, which is about stochastic difference equations taking values in a real or complex separable Banach space, to stochastic difference equations taking values in a complete subspace of a real or complex separable Banach space.

Lemma 2
.

Assume that Assumptions
1
-
4
and the conditions in Lemma
1
hold. Moreover, assume that for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
, there exists an
m_{j}>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}\sup_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}}\left|\nabla_{x_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|^{m_{j}}\right]<\infty.

Then,
(\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t}(\bm{\theta}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
given by

\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbf{F}_{t}(\mathbf{P}^{\prime}\bm{\pi}_{t-1\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta});\bm{\theta})\mathbf{P}^{\prime}\bm{\pi}_{t-1\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta})

where

[\mathbf{F}_{t}(\mathbf{s})]_{ii}:=\frac{f_{i}(Y_{t};X_{i,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{i}),\bm{\upsilon}_{i})}{\sum_{k=1}^{J}s_{k}f_{k}(Y_{t};X_{k,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k}),\bm{\upsilon}_{k})},\quad i\in\{1,...,J\}

is stationary and ergodic for all
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
and

\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}}\left|\left|\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{t\mid t}(\bm{\theta})-\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t}(\bm{\theta})\right|\right|_{2}\overset{e.a.s.}{\rightarrow}0\quad\text{as}\quad t\rightarrow\infty

for any initialisation
\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{0\mid 0}(\bm{\theta})\in\mathcal{S}
.

The next corollary is a direct consequence hereof.

Corollary 1
.

Under the assumptions in Lemma
2
,
(\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
given by

\bm{\pi}_{t+1\mid t}(\bm{\theta})=\mathbf{P}^{\prime}\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t}(\bm{\theta})

is stationary and ergodic for all
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
and

\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}}\left|\left|\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta})-\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta})\right|\right|_{2}\overset{e.a.s.}{\rightarrow}0\quad\text{as}\quad t\rightarrow\infty

for any initialisation
\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{0\mid 0}(\bm{\theta})\in\mathcal{S}
.

Remark 1
.

Note that, although
\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t}(\bm{\theta})\in\mathcal{S}
for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta})\in\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\theta}}
for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
where
\mathcal{S}_{\bm{\theta}}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{J}:x_{j}\geq\min_{i\in\{1,...,J\}}p_{ij},j=1,...,J,\sum_{j=1}^{J}x_{j}=1\}
.

The result in Lemma
2
/Corollary
1
is not surprising. The Markov chain itself forgets its initialisation asymptotically (in case it is initialised) as
p_{ij}>0
for all
i,j\in\{1,...,J\}
, so it is not surprising that the filter/predictor also forgets its initialisation asymptotically provided that the time-varying parameters do the same.

Moreover, we assume the following.

Assumption 5
.

For each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}|\log f_{j}(Y_{t};X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),\bm{\upsilon}_{j})|\right]<\infty.

Assumption
5
is a standard moment condition and is similar to Assumption 3 in
Douc et al. (2004)
and Assumption 4 in
Kasahara and Shimotsu (2019)
.

Finally, we assume the following as in
Francq and Roussignol (1998)
and
Francq et al. (2001)
where
f^{(m)}(\mathbf{y};\bm{\theta}),\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{Y}^{m}
denotes the conditional pdf of
\mathbf{Y}_{t-m+1}^{t}
given
\mathbf{Y}_{-\infty}^{t-m}
.

Assumption 6
.

There exists an
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle f^{(m)}(\mathbf{Y}_{t-m+1}^{t};\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=f^{(m)}(\mathbf{Y}_{t-m+1}^{t};\bm{\theta}_{0})\quad a.s.

implies that

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}

\displaystyle=\bm{\theta}_{0}.

Assumption
6
is a standard identification condition. Note that slightly different identification conditions are used in
Douc et al. (2004)
and
Kasahara and Shimotsu (2019)
.

Theorem
2
gives conditions under which the MLE is consistent.

Theorem 2
.

Assume that Assumptions
1
-
6
and the conditions in Lemmas
1
and
2
hold. Then,

\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{T}\overset{a.s.}{\rightarrow}\bm{\theta}_{0}\quad\text{as}\quad T\rightarrow\infty.

5.2
Asymptotic Normality

Asymptotic normality follows from classical arguments as consistency if the
initialised
and thus non-stationary score vector
\sqrt{T}\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\hat{L}_{T}(\bm{\theta}_{0})
and observed Fisher information matrix
(-\nabla_{\bm{\theta}\bm{\theta}}\hat{L}_{T}(\bm{\theta}))
converge in distribution to
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}(\bm{\theta}_{0}))
and uniformly a.s. to
\mathbf{I}(\bm{\theta})
, respectively, where

\mathbf{I}(\bm{\theta})=-\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{\bm{\theta}\bm{\theta}}\log f(Y_{t};\bm{\theta})]

is the Fisher information matrix. As above, we show both in two steps, the first steps being the main difficulties and hence focus. To show the former, we first show that the difference between
\sqrt{T}\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\hat{L}_{T}(\bm{\theta})
and the
non-initialised
, stationary, and ergodic score vector
\sqrt{T}\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}L_{T}(\bm{\theta})
converges uniformly a.s. to zero by showing that the first-order derivatives of the time-varying parameters and the predictor forget their initialisations asymptotically and then that
\sqrt{T}\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}L_{T}(\bm{\theta}_{0})
converges in distribution to
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}(\bm{\theta}_{0}))
by using standard arguments. To show the latter, we first show that the difference between
(-\nabla_{\bm{\theta}\bm{\theta}}\hat{L}_{T}(\bm{\theta}))
and the
non-initialised
, stationary, and ergodic observed Fisher information matrix
(-\nabla_{\bm{\theta}\bm{\theta}}L_{T}(\bm{\theta}))
converges uniformly a.s. to zero by showing that also the second-order derivatives of the time-varying parameters and the predictor forget their initialisations asymptotically and then that
(-\nabla_{\bm{\theta}\bm{\theta}}L_{T}(\bm{\theta}))
converges uniformly a.s. to
\mathbf{I}(\bm{\theta})
by using standard arguments.

In the following, let, for a function
\bm{\upsilon}\mapsto f(X(\bm{\upsilon}),\bm{\upsilon}):\bm{\Upsilon}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
where
\bm{\upsilon}\mapsto X(\bm{\upsilon}):\bm{\Upsilon}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is another function,
\bar{\nabla}_{x}f(X(\bm{\upsilon}),\bm{\upsilon})
and
\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}}f(X(\bm{\upsilon}),\bm{\upsilon})
be given by

\bar{\nabla}_{x}f(X(\bm{\upsilon}),\bm{\upsilon}):=\left.\nabla_{\bar{x}}f(\bar{x},\bar{\bm{\upsilon}})\right|_{\bar{x}=X(\bm{\upsilon}),\bar{\bm{\upsilon}}=\bm{\upsilon}}\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}}f(X(\bm{\upsilon}),\bm{\upsilon}):=\left.\nabla_{\bar{\bm{\upsilon}}}f(\bar{x},\bar{\bm{\upsilon}})\right|_{\bar{x}=X(\bm{\upsilon}),\bar{\bm{\upsilon}}=\bm{\upsilon}},

\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}x}f(X(\bm{\upsilon}),\bm{\upsilon})
be given by

\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}x}f(X(\bm{\upsilon}),\bm{\upsilon}):=\left.\nabla_{\bar{\bm{\upsilon}}\bar{x}}f(\bar{x},\bar{\bm{\upsilon}})\right|_{\bar{x}=X(\bm{\upsilon}),\bar{\bm{\upsilon}}=\bm{\upsilon}},

and
\bar{\nabla}_{xx}f(X(\bm{\upsilon}),\bm{\upsilon})
and
\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}\bm{\upsilon}}f(X(\bm{\upsilon}),\bm{\upsilon})
be given by

\bar{\nabla}_{xx}f(X(\bm{\upsilon}),\bm{\upsilon}):=\left.\nabla_{\bar{x}\bar{x}}f(\bar{x},\bar{\bm{\upsilon}})\right|_{\bar{x}=X(\bm{\upsilon}),\bar{\bm{\upsilon}}=\bm{\upsilon}}\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}\bm{\upsilon}}f(X(\bm{\upsilon}),\bm{\upsilon}):=\left.\nabla_{\bar{\bm{\upsilon}}\bar{\bm{\upsilon}}}f(\bar{x},\bar{\bm{\upsilon}})\right|_{\bar{x}=X(\bm{\upsilon}),\bar{\bm{\upsilon}}=\bm{\upsilon}}.

In addition to Assumptions
1
-
6
, we assume the following.

Assumption 7
.

\bm{\theta}_{0}\in\textup{int}(\bm{\Theta})
.

Assumption 8
.

For each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

(i)

(x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\mapsto f_{j}(y;x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})
is twice continuously differentiable for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
and

(ii)

x_{j}\mapsto\nabla_{(x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})(x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}f_{j}(y;x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})
is differentiable for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
and
\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}
.

Assumption 9
.

For each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

(i)

(x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\mapsto\phi_{j}(y,x_{j};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})
is twice continuously differentiable for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
.

Assumption
7
is a standard regularity condition, which implies that there exists an
\varepsilon>0
such that
\bm{\theta}_{0}\in\textup{int}(\bar{\bm{\Theta}})
where

\bar{\bm{\Theta}}:=\left\{\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}:\left|\left|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}_{0}\right|\right|_{2}\leq\varepsilon\right\}\subset\textup{int}(\bm{\Theta}).

Assumptions
8
and
9
are also standard regularity conditions; Condition (i) in Assumption
8
is similar to Assumption 6 in
Douc et al. (2004)
and Assumption 7(a) in
Kasahara and Shimotsu (2019)
, and Condition (ii) in Assumption
8
is an extra regularity condition, which is used to prove that the second-order derivative of the predictor forgets its initialisation asymptotically.

The next two lemmata, which follow from applications of Theorem 2.10 in
Straumann and Mikosch (2006)
, give conditions under which, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
, the difference between
(\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}))_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
and the one between
(\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}))_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
converge uniformly e.a.s. to zero.

Lemma 3
.

Assume that Assumptions
1
,
2
,
4
,
9
, and the conditions in Lemma
1
hold. Moreover, assume that, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

(i)

\mathbb{E}[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}]<\infty
.

Then, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,
(\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is stationary and ergodic for all
\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}
. Finally, assume that, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

(i)

\mathbb{E}[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}]<\infty
,

(ii)

\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})-\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}\overset{e.a.s.}{\rightarrow}0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
, and

(iii)

\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}|\bar{\nabla}_{x_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})-\bar{\nabla}_{x_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})|\overset{e.a.s.}{\rightarrow}0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
.

Then, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})-\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}\overset{e.a.s.}{\rightarrow}0\quad\text{as}\quad t\rightarrow\infty

for any initialisation
\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\hat{X}_{j,1}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{j}}
.

Lemma 4
.

Assume that Assumptions
1
,
2
,
4
,
9
, and the conditions in Lemmas
1
and
3
hold. Moreover, assume that, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

(i)

\mathbb{E}[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}|\bar{\nabla}_{x_{j}x_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})|]<\infty
,

(ii)

\mathbb{E}[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}x_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}]<\infty
, and

(iii)

\mathbb{E}[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2,2}]<\infty
.

Then, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,
(\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is stationary and ergodic for all
\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}
. Finally, assume that, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

(i)

\mathbb{E}[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2,2}]<\infty
,

(ii)

\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}|\bar{\nabla}_{x_{j}x_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})-\bar{\nabla}_{x_{j}x_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})|\overset{e.a.s.}{\rightarrow}0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
,

(iii)

\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}x_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})-\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}x_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}\overset{e.a.s.}{\rightarrow}0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
, and

(iv)

\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})-\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2,2}\overset{e.a.s.}{\rightarrow}0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
.

Then, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})-\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2,2}\overset{e.a.s.}{\rightarrow}0\quad\text{as}\quad t\rightarrow\infty

for any initialisation
\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\hat{X}_{j,1}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{j}\times d_{j}}
.

We also assume the following.

Assumption 10
.

For each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

(i)

\mathbb{E}[\sup\nolimits_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}|\bar{\nabla}_{x_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),\bm{\upsilon}_{j})|^{2}||\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}^{2}]<\infty
,

(ii)

\mathbb{E}[\sup\nolimits_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}^{2}]<\infty
,

(iii)

\mathbb{E}[\sup\nolimits_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}|\bar{\nabla}_{x_{j}x_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),\bm{\upsilon}_{j})|||\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}^{2}]<\infty
,

(iv)

\mathbb{E}[\sup\nolimits_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}|\bar{\nabla}_{x_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),\bm{\upsilon}_{j})|||\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2,2}]<\infty
,

(v)

\mathbb{E}[\sup\nolimits_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}x_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}||\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}]<\infty
, and

(vi)

\mathbb{E}[\sup\nolimits_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2,2}]<\infty
.

Assumption
10
is standard moment conditions, which imply that, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

\mathbb{E}[\sup\nolimits_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}^{2}]<\infty

and

\mathbb{E}[\sup\nolimits_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2,2}]<\infty,

and is thus similar to Assumption 7(b) in
Douc et al. (2004)
and Assumption 7(c) in
Kasahara and Shimotsu (2019)
.

In the following two lemmata, let, for a function
\mathbf{x}\mapsto\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x}):\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})
be given by

[\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})]_{(j-1)n+i}=\nabla_{x_{i}}f_{j}(\mathbf{x}),\quad(i,j)\in\{1,...,n\}\times\{1,...,k\}

and
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})
be given by

[\nabla_{\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})]_{(j-1)n^{2}+(i-1)n+l}=\nabla_{x_{i}x_{l}}f_{j}(\mathbf{x}),\quad(i,j,l)\in\{1,...,n\}\times\{1,...,k\}\times\{1,...,n\}.

With this notation, the next two lemmata, which also follow from applications of Theorem 2.10 in
Straumann and Mikosch (2006)
, give conditions under which the difference between
(\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta}))_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
and the one between
(\nabla_{\bm{\theta}\bm{\theta}}\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta}))_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\nabla_{\bm{\theta}\bm{\theta}}\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
converge uniformly e.a.s. to zero.

Lemma 5
.

Assume that Assumptions
1
-
4
,
8
-
10
, and the conditions in Lemmas
1
-
3
hold. Moreover, assume that for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
, there exists an
m_{j}>0
such that

(i)

\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\sup_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}}||\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}^{m_{j}}]<\infty
,

(ii)

\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\sup_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}}|\bar{\nabla}_{x_{j}x_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})|^{m_{j}}]<\infty
, and

(iii)

\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\sup_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}}||\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}x_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}^{m_{j}}]<\infty
.

Then,
(\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is stationary and ergodic for all
\bm{\theta}\in\bar{\bm{\Theta}}
with
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\bar{\bm{\Theta}}}||\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta})||_{2}^{2}]<\infty
and

\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\bar{\bm{\Theta}}}||\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta})-\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta})||_{2}\overset{e.a.s.}{\rightarrow}0\quad\text{as}\quad t\rightarrow\infty

for any initialisation
\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{0\mid 0}(\bm{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}^{(J-1)d}
.

Lemma 6
.

Assume that Assumptions
1
-
4
,
8
-
10
, and the conditions in Lemmas
1
-
5
hold. Moreover, assume that for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
, there exists an
m_{j}>0
such that

(i)

\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\sup_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}}||\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2,2}^{m_{j}}]<\infty
,

(ii)

\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\sup_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}}|\bar{\nabla}_{x_{j}x_{j}x_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})|^{m_{j}}]<\infty
,

(iii)

\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\sup_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}}||\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}x_{j}x_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}^{m_{j}}]<\infty
, and

(iv)

\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\sup_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}}||\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}x_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2,2}^{m_{j}}]<\infty
.

Then,
(\nabla_{\bm{\theta}\bm{\theta}}\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta}))_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is stationary and ergodic for all
\bm{\theta}\in\bar{\bm{\Theta}}
with
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\bar{\bm{\Theta}}}||\nabla_{\bm{\theta}\bm{\theta}}\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta})||_{2}]<\infty
and

\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\bar{\bm{\Theta}}}||\nabla_{\bm{\theta}\bm{\theta}}\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta})-\nabla_{\bm{\theta}\bm{\theta}}\bm{\pi}_{t\mid t-1}(\bm{\theta})||_{2}\overset{e.a.s.}{\rightarrow}0\quad\text{as}\quad t\rightarrow\infty

for any initialisation
\nabla_{\bm{\theta}\bm{\theta}}\hat{\bm{\pi}}_{0\mid 0}(\bm{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}^{(J-1)d^{2}}
.

Theorem
3
gives conditions under which the MLE is also asymptotically normal.

Theorem 3
.

Assume that Assumptions
1
-
10
and the conditions in Lemmas
1
-
6
hold and that
\mathbf{I}(\bm{\theta}_{0})
is invertible. Then,

\sqrt{T}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{T}-\bm{\theta}_{0})\overset{d}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}(\bm{\theta}_{0})^{-1})\quad\text{as}\quad T\rightarrow\infty.

Douc et al. (2004)
also assume that the Fisher information matrix
\mathbf{I}(\bm{\theta}_{0})
is invertible; an assumption which seems to be missing in
Kasahara and Shimotsu (2019)
.

Finally, Proposition
1
shows that, under the assumptions in Theorem
3
, the observed Fisher information matrix
(-\nabla_{\bm{\theta}\bm{\theta}}\hat{L}_{T}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{T}))
is a consistent estimator of the Fisher information matrix
\mathbf{I}(\bm{\theta}_{0})
.

Proposition 1
.

Under the assumptions in Theorem
3
,

\nabla_{\bm{\theta}\bm{\theta}}\hat{L}_{T}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{T})\overset{a.s.}{\rightarrow}(-\mathbf{I}(\bm{\theta}_{0}))\quad\text{as}\quad T\rightarrow\infty.

6
The Markov-switching GARCH Model

In this section, we study both the asymptotic and finite-sample properties of the MLE for the Markov-switching GARCH model by
Haas et al. (2004b)
, the latter in a Monte Carlo simulation study.
8
8

8

Recall that the Markov-switching GARCH model reduces to the mixture GARCH model by
Haas et al. (2004a)
if
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an i.i.d. chain.

6.1
Asymptotic Properties

Recall that the Markov-switching GARCH model by
Haas et al. (2004b)
is given by

Y_{t}=\sqrt{X_{S_{t},t}}\varepsilon_{t},

where
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a stationary, irreducible, and aperiodic (thus ergodic) Markov chain with transition probabilities
p_{ij,0}\in(0,1),i,j\in\{1,...,J\}
, for each
j\in\{1,...,J\}
,

X_{j,t+1}=\omega_{j,0}+\alpha_{j,0}Y_{t}^{2}+\beta_{j,0}X_{j,t},

where
\omega_{j,0}>0
,
\alpha_{j,0}\geq 0
, and
\beta_{j,0}\geq 0
,
(\varepsilon_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence of independent normal distributed random variables with zero mean and unit variance, and
(S_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
(\varepsilon_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
are independent.

Liu (2006)
gave conditions under which the model is stationary and ergodic. In the following,
\mathbf{M}_{0}
is a
J^{2}\times J^{2}
matrix given by

\left[\mathbf{M}_{0}\right]_{ij}:=p_{ji,0}\left(\bm{\alpha}_{0}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\prime}+\bm{\beta}_{0}\right),\quad i,j\in\{1,...,J\},

where
\bm{\alpha}_{0}:=(\alpha_{1,0},...,\alpha_{J,0})^{\prime}
,
\bm{\beta}_{0}:=\textup{diag}(\beta_{1,0},...,\beta_{J,0})
, and
\mathbf{e}_{i}
is the
i
’th unit vector in
\mathbb{R}^{J}
.

Theorem 4

(
Liu (2006)
)
.

(Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is stationary and ergodic with
\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}^{2}]<\infty
if and only if
\rho(\mathbf{M}_{0})<1
where
\rho(\mathbf{M}_{0})
is the spectral radius of
\mathbf{M}_{0}
.

Note that
\rho(\mathbf{M}_{0})<1
does not imply that
\alpha_{j,0}+\beta_{j,0}<1
for all
j\in\{1,...,J\}
, see
Liu (2006)
.

We now give conditions under which the MLE is consistent and asymptotically normal. The MLE is consistent under the following assumptions.

Assumption 11
.

\bm{\theta}_{0}\in\bm{\Theta}
.

Assumption 12
.

\rho(\mathbf{M}_{0})<1
.

Assumption 13
.

\bm{\Theta}
is compact.

Assumption 14
.

For all
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
,
\beta_{j}<1
for all
j\in\{1,...,J\}
.

Assumption 15
.

For all
\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}
, there exists an
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
f^{(m)}(\mathbf{Y}_{t-m+1}^{t};\bm{\theta})=f^{(m)}(\mathbf{Y}_{t-m+1}^{t};\bm{\theta}_{0})
a.s. implies that
\bm{\theta}=\bm{\theta}_{0}
.

Theorem 5
.

If Assumptions
11
-
15
hold, then

\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{T}\overset{a.s.}{\rightarrow}\bm{\theta}_{0}\quad\text{as}\quad T\rightarrow\infty.

Proof.

In the following,
\underline{\bm{\theta}}=\inf_{\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}}\bm{\theta}
and
\overline{\bm{\theta}}=\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\bm{\Theta}}\bm{\theta}
. Note that
(Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is stationary and ergodic by Theorem
4
. We therefore only need to verify Assumptions
2
-
6
and the conditions in Lemmas
1
and
2
.

Let
j\in\{1,...,J\}
be given. First, Assumption
2
is true by assumption and Assumptions
3
and
4
are trivially satisfied.

We now verify the conditions in Lemmas
1
and
2
. First, by Lemma 2.2 in
Straumann and Mikosch (2006)
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}\left|\phi_{j}(Y_{t},x_{j};\bm{\upsilon}_{j})-x_{j}\right|\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}\left|\omega_{j}+\alpha_{j}Y_{t}^{2}+\beta_{j}x_{j}-x_{j}\right|\right]\leq C_{j}+2\mathbb{E}\left[\log^{+}\left|Y_{t}\right|\right]

for all
x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}
where
C_{j}=6\log 2+\log^{+}\overline{\omega}_{j}+\log^{+}\overline{\alpha}_{j}+\log^{+}\overline{\beta}_{j}+2\log^{+}x_{j}<\infty
, so Condition (i) in Lemma
1
is satisfied since
\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}^{2}]<\infty
implies that
\mathbb{E}[\log^{+}|Y_{t}|]<\infty
by Lemma 2.2 in
Straumann and Mikosch (2006)
. Moreover,

\mathbb{E}\left[\log\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}\Lambda_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\log\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}\beta_{j}\right]=\log\overline{\beta}_{j},

so Conditions (ii) and (iii) in Lemma
1
are also satisfied. Finally,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}\sup_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}}\left|\nabla_{x_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}\sup_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}}\left|-\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{x_{j}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{Y_{t}^{2}}{x_{j}^{2}}\right|\right]\leq\frac{1}{2\underline{x}_{j}}+\frac{1}{2\underline{x}_{j}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t}^{2}\right],

where
\underline{x}_{j}=\frac{\underline{\omega}_{j}}{1-\underline{\beta}_{j}}>0
, so the condition in Lemma
2
is also satisfied since
\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}^{2}]<\infty
.

Moreover, note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}\left|\log f_{j}(Y_{t};X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}\left|-\frac{1}{2}\log 2\pi-\frac{1}{2}\log X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})-\frac{1}{2}\frac{Y_{t}^{2}}{X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\log 2\pi+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}\left|\log X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\right]+\frac{1}{2\underline{x}_{j}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t}^{2}\right],

so Assumption
5
is also satisfied since
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}|\log X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})|]<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}^{2}]<\infty
. Indeed,
\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}^{2}]<\infty
implies that
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}|\log X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})|]<\infty
, which we now show. Note that
\log x=\log^{+}x-\log^{-}x
for all
x>0
where
\log^{+}x=\max(\log x,0)
and
\log^{-}x=-\min(\log x,0)
. Thus,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}\left|\log X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right]+\log^{-}\underline{x}_{j}.

Now, by Lemma
1
,

X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})=\frac{\omega_{j}}{1-\beta_{j}}+\alpha_{j}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\beta_{j}^{i}Y_{t-1-i}^{2}\quad a.s.

(7)

Thus,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right]\leq\frac{\overline{\omega}_{j}}{1-\overline{\beta}_{j}}+\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{j}}{1-\overline{\beta}_{j}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t-1}^{2}\right].

Hence,
\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}^{2}]<\infty
implies that
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}|\log X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})|]<\infty
since
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})]<\infty
implies that
\mathbb{E}[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bm{\Upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})]<\infty
by Lemma 2.2 in
Straumann and Mikosch (2006)
. Finally, Assumption
6
is true by assumption.
∎

Assumptions
11
-
15
are identical to the assumptions in
Kandji and Misko (2024)
, except that
Kandji and Misko (2024)
assume that Assumption
15
holds with
m=1
.

Although the above result is identical to the one in
Kandji and Misko (2024)
, the proof of it is different. To prove consistency of the MLE,
Kandji and Misko (2024)
use one representation of the likelihood function, which is similar to the one used in
Francq and Roussignol (1998)
and
Francq et al. (2001)
(see also Equation (12.25) in
Francq and Zakoïan (2019)
), whereas we use another representation of the likelihood function, which is similar to the one used in
Douc et al. (2004)
and
Kasahara and Shimotsu (2019)
(see also Equation (12.30) in
Francq and Zakoïan (2019)
). The advantage of the latter is that it can also be used to prove asymptotic normality of the MLE, which we now do.

To give conditions under which the MLE is also asymptotically normal, we need the following result where, for all
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{(\otimes s)}
is a
J^{s+1}\times J^{s+1}
matrix given by

\left[\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{(\otimes s)}\right]_{ij}:=p_{ji,0}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{A}_{it,0}^{(\otimes s)}\right],\quad i,j\in\{1,...,J\}

with

\mathbf{A}_{it,0}:=\varepsilon_{t}^{2}\bm{\alpha}_{0}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\prime}+\bm{\beta}_{0},

where
\otimes
denotes the Kronecker product.

Theorem 6

(
Liu (2006)
)
.

If
\rho(\mathbf{M}_{0})<1
and
\rho(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{(\otimes s)})<1
, then
\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}^{2s}]<\infty
.

In addition to the above assumptions, the MLE is asymptotically normal under the following assumptions.

Assumption 16
.

\bm{\theta}_{0}\in\textup{int}(\bm{\Theta})
.

Assumption 17
.

\rho(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{(\otimes 3)})<1
.

Theorem 7
.

If Assumptions
11
-
17
hold and
\mathbf{I}(\bm{\theta}_{0})
is invertible, then

\sqrt{T}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{T}-\bm{\theta}_{0})\overset{d}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I}(\bm{\theta}_{0})^{-1})\quad\text{as}\quad T\rightarrow\infty.

Proof.

In the following,
\underline{\bm{\theta}}=\inf_{\bm{\theta}\in\bar{\bm{\Theta}}}\bm{\theta}
and
\overline{\bm{\theta}}=\sup_{\bm{\theta}\in\bar{\bm{\Theta}}}\bm{\theta}
. We need to verify Assumptions
7
-
10
and the conditions in Lemmas
3
-
6
.

Let, as in the proof of Theorem
5
,
j\in\{1,...,J\}
be given. First, Assumption
7
is true by assumption and Assumptions
8
and
9
are trivially satisfied.

We now verify the conditions in Lemma
3
. First,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\left|\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\right|_{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left(1+Y_{t}^{2}+X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4\log 2+2\mathbb{E}\left[\log^{+}\left|Y_{t}\right|\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right],

so Condition (i) is satisfied since
\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}^{6}]<\infty
implies that
\mathbb{E}[\log^{+}|Y_{t}|]<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})]<\infty
, see the proof of Theorem
5
. We now show that
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}^{2}]<\infty
, which implies that
\mathbb{E}[\log^{+}\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}]<\infty
. Note that

\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\beta_{j}^{i}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
Y_{t-1-i}^{2}\\
X_{j,t-1-i}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\end{bmatrix}\quad a.s.,

so, by Minkowskis inequality,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\left|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\right|_{2}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{1}{1-\overline{\beta}_{j}}+\frac{1}{1-\overline{\beta}_{j}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t-1}^{4}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}+\frac{1}{1-\overline{\beta}_{j}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}X_{j,t-1}^{2}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Hence,
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}^{2}]<\infty
since
\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}^{6}]<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}X_{j,t}^{2}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})]<\infty
, see the proof of Theorem
5
. Finally,

\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\left|\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})-\bar{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\right|_{2}=\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})-X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|

and

\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\bar{\nabla}_{x_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})-\bar{\nabla}_{x_{j}}\phi_{j}(Y_{t},X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j});\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|=0,

so Conditions (ii) and (iii) are also satisfied since
\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}|\hat{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})-X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})|\overset{e.a.s.}{\rightarrow}0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
. The conditions in Lemma
4
can be verified similarly.

Moving on to Assumption
10
, by Hölders inequality, for all
m\in(1,\frac{6}{4}]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\bar{\nabla}_{x_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|^{2}\left|\left|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\right|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{Y_{t}^{2}}{X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\right|^{2}\left|\left|\frac{\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}{X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\right|\right|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{Y_{t}^{2}}{X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\right|^{2m}\right]^{\frac{1}{m}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\left|\frac{\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}{X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\right|\right|_{2}^{2m^{c}}\right]^{\frac{1}{m^{c}}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{\underline{x}_{j}^{2m}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|Y_{t}\right|^{4m}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{m}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\left|\frac{\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}{X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\right|\right|_{2}^{2m^{c}}\right]^{\frac{1}{m^{c}}}

with
\underline{x}_{j}=\frac{\underline{\omega}_{j}}{1-\underline{\beta}_{j}}
and
m^{c}=\frac{m}{m-1}
where the last inequality follows from the inequality
|x+y|^{p}\leq 2^{p}|x|^{p}+2^{p}|y|^{p}
for all
x,y\in(-\infty,\infty)
and
p\in(0,\infty)
. Note that, by Equation (
7
), for all
n\in(0,\frac{1}{m^{c}})
,

\displaystyle\frac{\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}{X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{1-\beta_{j}}\frac{1}{X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\\
\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\beta_{j}^{i}Y_{t-1-i}^{2}\right)\frac{1}{X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\\
\left(\frac{\omega_{j}}{(1-\beta_{j})^{2}}+\alpha_{j}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}i\beta_{j}^{i-1}Y_{t-1-i}^{2}\right)\frac{1}{X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\leq\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{\omega_{j}}\\
\frac{1}{\alpha_{j}}\\
\frac{1}{1-\beta_{j}}+\frac{1}{\beta_{j}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}i\frac{\alpha_{j}\beta_{j}^{i}Y_{t-1-i}^{2}}{\frac{\omega_{j}}{1-\beta_{j}}+\alpha_{j}\beta_{j}^{i}Y_{t-1-i}^{2}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\leq\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{\omega_{j}}\\
\frac{1}{\alpha_{j}}\\
\frac{1}{1-\beta_{j}}+\frac{1}{\omega_{j}^{n}}\alpha_{j}^{n}\frac{(1-\beta_{j})^{n}}{\beta_{j}}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}i\beta_{j}^{ni}\left|Y_{t-1-i}\right|^{2n}\end{bmatrix}\quad a.s.,

where the inequalities are elementwise and the last one follows from the inequality
\frac{x}{1+x}\leq x^{p}
for all
x\in[0,\infty)
and
p\in(0,1)
, so, by Minkowskis inequality,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\left|\frac{\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}{X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\right|\right|_{2}^{2m^{c}}\right]^{\frac{1}{2m^{c}}}\leq A_{j}+B_{j}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|Y_{t-1}\right|^{4nm^{c}}\right]^{\frac{1}{2m^{c}}},

where
A_{j}=\frac{1}{\underline{\omega}_{j}}+\frac{1}{\underline{\alpha}_{j}}+\frac{1}{1-\overline{\beta}_{j}}
and
B_{j}=\frac{1}{\underline{\omega}_{j}^{n}}\overline{\alpha}_{j}^{n}\frac{(1-\underline{\beta}_{j})^{n}\overline{\beta}_{j}^{n}}{\underline{\beta}_{j}(1-\overline{\beta}_{j}^{n})^{2}}
. Hence,
\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\left|\frac{\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}{X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\right|\right|_{2}^{2m^{c}}\right]<\infty
since
\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}^{6}]<\infty
. The first condition in Assumption
10
is thus satisfied since
\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}^{6}]<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\left|\frac{\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}{X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\right|\right|_{2}^{2m^{c}}\right]<\infty
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\bar{\nabla}_{x_{j}x_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\left|\left|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\right|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\frac{1}{2}-\frac{Y_{t}^{2}}{{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\right|\left|\left|\frac{\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}{{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\right|\right|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\frac{1}{2}-\frac{Y_{t}^{2}}{{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\right|^{m}\right]^{\frac{1}{m}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\left|\frac{\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}{{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\right|\right|_{2}^{2m^{c}}\right]^{\frac{1}{m^{c}}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{2^{m}}{\underline{x}_{j}^{m}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|Y_{t}\right|^{2m}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{m}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\left|\frac{\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}{{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\right|\right|_{2}^{2m^{c}}\right]^{\frac{1}{m^{c}}},

so the second condition in Assumption
10
is also satisfied. Finally, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\bar{\nabla}_{x_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};{X}_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j}),\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\left|\left|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\right|_{2,2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|-\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{Y_{t}^{2}}{X_{j,t}^{2}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\right|\left|\left|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\right|_{2,2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|-\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{Y_{t}^{2}}{X_{j,t}^{2}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})}\right|^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\left|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\right|_{2,2}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{\underline{x}_{j}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\underline{x}_{j}^{4}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t}^{4}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\left|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\right|_{2,2}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}},

where the last inequality follows from the inequality
|x+y|^{p}\leq 2^{p}|x|^{p}+2^{p}|y|^{p}
for all
x,y\in(-\infty,\infty)
and
p\in(0,\infty)
as above. Note that

\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\beta_{j}^{i}\left(\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\begin{bmatrix}0&0&1\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}^{\prime}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right)\quad a.s.,

so, by Minkowskis inequality,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\left|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\right|_{2,2}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{2}{1-\overline{\beta}_{j}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\left|\left|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\right|_{2}^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Hence,
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2,2}^{2}]<\infty
since
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2}^{2}]<\infty
. The third condition in Assumption
10
is thus also satisfied since
\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}^{6}]<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}||\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\bm{\upsilon}_{j}}X_{j,t}(\bm{\upsilon}_{j})||_{2,2}^{2}]<\infty
.

Finally, we verify the condition in Lemma
5
. Note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\sup_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}}\left|\bar{\nabla}_{x_{j}x_{j}}\log f_{j}(Y_{t};x_{j},\bm{\upsilon}_{j})\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\bm{\upsilon}_{j}\in\bar{\bm{\Upsilon}}_{j}}\sup_{x_{j}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}}\left|\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{x_{j}^{2}}-\frac{Y_{t}^{2}}{x_{j}^{3}}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\underline{x}_{j}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\underline{x}_{j}^{3}}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{t}^{2}\right],

so that condition is also satisfied since
\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}^{6}]<\infty
. The condition in Lemma
6
can be verified similarly.
∎

Remark 2
.

An inspection of the proof of Theorem
7
shows that we only need to assume that there exists an
\varepsilon>0
such that
\mathbb{E}[|Y_{t}|^{4+\varepsilon}]<\infty
. However, in the literature, there exist to the best of our knowledge no conditions under which this is directly the case. We thus assume that
\rho(\bm{\Sigma}_{0}^{(\otimes 3)})<1
, which implies that
\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}^{6}]<\infty
, a stronger assumption than the assumption that there exists an
\varepsilon>0
such that
\mathbb{E}[|Y_{t}|^{4+\varepsilon}]<\infty
.

6.2
Finite-sample Properties

To study the finite-sample properties of the MLE for the Markov-switching GARCH model by
Haas et al. (2004b)
, we perform a Monte Carlo simulation study.

Tables
1
and
2
report the estimated means and standard deviations (in parentheses) of the estimated parameters of a range of two-state Markov-switching GARCH models together with the true parameters. The benchmark model is a two-state Markov-switching GARCH model with true parameters
\omega_{1,0}=0.025
,
\alpha_{1,0}=0.05
,
\beta_{1,0}=0.90
,
\omega_{2,0}=0.25
,
\alpha_{2,0}=0.30
,
\beta_{2,0}=0.60
,
p_{11,0}=0.95
, and
p_{22,0}=0.90
